> [L1] With the new way, you are making more money and getting more customers. This means that your cost to acquire a customer is so cheap (relative to how much you make) that your limiting factor becomes your ability to do the work you already love doing. Cash flow and acquiring customers is no longer your bottleneck because it’s 22.4x more profitable than the old model. Yup. You read that right. This is the part in the action movie where you walk away from an explosion in slow motion. This is the exact Grand Slam Offer we used with our software business that serves agencies. The numbers can become wild . . . fast. 
> [L2] 
> [L3] I know 22.4x better sounds unreasonable, but that's the point. If you play the same game everyone else does, you’ll get the same results everyone else does (mediocre). When you get this right, the results are, well . . . unbelievable. Summary Points 
> [L4] ●The problem with commoditization and being the same as others 
> [L5] ●Price-based buying decisions vs Value-based buying decisions 
> [L6] ●The value of a grand slam offer is that you aren’t compared to anything else available in the marketplace 
> [L7] ●Real world impact of making a grand slam offer 
> [L8] 
> [L9] Problem #2: Bad Customers —> Solution #2: A Starving Crowd The seed that fell on good soil represents those who truly hear and understand God’s word and produce a harvest of thirty, sixty, or even a hundred times as much as had been planted!” - Matthew 13:23 (NLT)     
> [L10] 
> [L11] A grand slam offer presented to the wrong audience will fall on deaf ears. On the other hand, if you get the market right, you can get everything else wrong and still make money. This chapter focuses on picking the right market. Half-Page Story A marketing professor asked his students, “If you were going to open a hotdog stand, and you could only have one advantage over your competitors . . . which would it be . . . ?” “Location! ….Quality! …. Low prices! ….Best taste!” The students kept going until eventually they had run out of answers. They looked at each other waiting for the professor to speak. The room finally fell quiet. The professor smiled and replied, “A starving crowd.” You could have the worst hot dogs, terrible prices, and be in a terrible location, but if you’re the only hot dog stand in town and the local college football game breaks out, you’re going to sell out. That’s the value of a starving crowd. At the end of the day, if there is a ton of demand for a solution, you can be mediocre at business, have a terrible offer, and have no ability to persuade people, and you can still make money. An example of this was the toilet paper shortage at the beginning of Covid-19. 
> [L12] 
> [L13] There was no offer. The pricing was atrocious. And there was no compelling sales pitch. But because the crowd was so big and so starving, rolls of toilet paper were going for $100 or more. That’s the value of a starving crowd. Case Study: Selling Newspapers Bad Customer Example: One of my friends, Lloyd, owned a software company that converted print advertising products for newspapers into digital ads. He was modernizing an ancient business. He struggled mightily to grow it despite being a great entrepreneur. Main reason: the newspaper industry was shrinking 25 percent per year! No matter how good you are, the market is always the strongest lever for your success. Starving Crowd Example: On the flip side, even if you are a terrible, unskilled entrepreneur, if you were selling toilet paper during COVID, you were making money.
> # 【3†HOW TO MAKE A GRAND SLAM OFFER Prob.txt†file_00000000a8e471f5ad43f1268dce94ce】
> Content source: Source.file
> Title: HOW TO MAKE A GRAND SLAM OFFER Prob.txt
> Citation Marker: fileciteturn1file3
> Mclick Target: "1:3"
> Content Snippet:
> [L1] So if you want to have a higher success percentage, raise your prices. Summary Points 1)Charge a premium price because: a)No one wins in a race to the bottom. b)You can use the extra profits to make your thing better than everyone else’s. c)You get better customers d)You attract better talent e)You are more convicted f)They get more emotionally bought in g)They’ll automatically perceive you as higher value (wine example) 2)Don’t be afraid.
> [L2] 
> [L3] Problem #4: Your Thing Isn’t Valuable —> Solution #4: Make It Valuable Value Offer: The Value Equation “We question all of our beliefs, except for the ones we really believe in, and those we never think to question.”  -Orson Scott Card Value Equation:   To charge lots of money, you need to provide even more value. The equation above shows the relationship between the four value drivers. Two of the drivers (on top), you want to increase. The other two (on the bottom), you want to decrease. (1)(Yay) The Dream Outcome (Goal: Increase) (2)(Yay) Perceived Likelihood of Achievement (Goal: Increase) (3)(Boo) Perceived Time Delay Between Start and Achievement (Goal: Decrease) (4)(Boo) Perceived Effort & Sacrifice (Goal: Decrease) These value drivers typically correspond with the questions you will hear from a prospect when they are trying to figure out if your offer is “worth it”: What will I make? and/or What will happen? (Dream Outcome) How will I know it's going to happen? (Perceived Likelihood of Achievement) How long will it take? (Time Delay) What is expected of me? (Effort & Sacrifice) Get The Bottom To Zero Newbies focus on promising bigger and showing more testimonials. Pros focus on making things effortless and immediate. Reason: No matter how small the top side is, anything divided by zero equals infinity (which is technically undefined for the math nerds). In other words, if you can reduce your prospects' true time delay to receiving value to zero (aka you realize your immediate dream outcome), and your effort and sacrifice is zero, you have an infinitely valuable product. If you accomplish this, you win the game. Given this concept, a prospect would (in theory) purchase something from you, and the moment their credit card was run, it would immediately become their reality. That is infinite value. Imagine clicking the purchase button on a weight loss product and instantly seeing your stomach turn into a six-pack. Or imagine hiring a marketing firm, and as soon as you sign your document, your phone begins ringing with new highly qualified prospects. How valuable would these products/services be? Infinitely valuable. And that’s the point.  
> [L4] 
> [L5] Exercise #8: Which of the following apply to “getting the bottom to zero”? If you haven’t already, please scan the QR code to download the exercises for this book  ​ Perception is Reality For all value drivers, it’s not about what you think they are, or about what they are, but about what your prospect thinks they are. So you need to communicate the value drivers in language they can understand so they can perceive the value. Example: London Tunnel System The biggest increase in rider satisfaction (aka value) was never from faster trains to decrease wait times. Instead, it was from a simple dotted map that showed them when the next train was coming and how long they had to wait. The dotted map, which only cost a few million dollars, decreased the riders’ perception of time delay and sacrifice (being bored waiting) more than actually making the trains faster (which costs billions of dollars to do). Isn’t that cool?
> # 【4†HOW TO MAKE A GRAND SLAM OFFER Prob.txt†file_00000000a8e471f5ad43f1268dce94ce】
> Content source: Source.file
> Title: HOW TO MAKE A GRAND SLAM OFFER Prob.txt
> Citation Marker: fileciteturn1file4
> Mclick Target: "1:4"
> Content Snippet:
> [L1] The next month, the $5,000 that comes in would be your first profitable month, and each month thereafter would be profitable (assuming they all stay). This is an example of a commoditized service — normal agency work. There’s a million of them, and they all look the same. Commoditized businesses and offers have a harder time getting responses from ads because all their marketing looks the same as everyone else's.   It’s reasonable, but it’s easily duplicated (and subject to commoditization). This commoditization creates a price-driven purchase . . . 
> [L2] 
> [L3] You are forced to be priced “competitively” to get clients and to stay that way to keep them. If the client sees a cheaper version of the “same thing,” then the value discrepancy will cause them to swap providers. This is a dilemma . . . 
> [L4] 
> [L5] lose this client, the rest of your clients, and potential clients, or stay “competitive.” Your margins become so thin they vanish. 
> [L6] 
> [L7] Furthermore, it’s hard to get prospects to say yes (and keep them saying yes) unless you’re hypervigilant about clients commoditizing your business by staying “competitive.” And that’s the problem with the old commoditized way. They’re able to compare. 
> [L8] 
> [L9] Unless you switch to a Grand Slam Offer, your prices will keep getting beaten down. The business eventually dies, or the entrepreneur throws in the towel. No bueno. We want to make an offer that’s so different that you can skip the awkward explanation of why your product is different from everyone else’s (which, if they have to ask, then they are probably too ignorant to understand the explanation) and instead just have the offer do that work for you. That’s the Grand Slam Offer way. Let’s dive in to see the contrast in sales numbers. New Grand Slam Offer Way (Differentiated, Incomparable) (Value-Driven) Grand Slam Offer: Pay one time. (No recurring fee. No retainer.) Just cover ad spend. I’ll generate leads and work your leads for you. And only pay me if people show up. And I’ll guarantee you get 20 people in your first month, or you get your next month free. I’ll also provide all the best practices from other businesses like yours. 
> [L10] 
> [L11] ●Daily sales coaching for your staff 
> [L12] ●Tested scripts 
> [L13] ●Tested price points and offers to swipe and deploy 
> [L14] ●Sales recordings . . . and everything else you need to sell and fulfill your customers. 
> [L15] 
> [L16] I’ll give you the entire play book for (insert industry), absolutely free just for becoming a client. In a nutshell, I'm feeding people into your business, showing you, exactly, how to sell them so that you can get the highest prices, which means that you make the most money possible . . . sound fair enough? 
> [L17] 
> [L18] It’s clear these are drastically different offers . . . but so what? Where’s the money!? Let’s compare both in the below chart.       Breakdown: You spend the same amount of money for the same eyeballs. Then, you get 2.5x more people to respond to your advertisement because it’s a more compelling offer. From there, you close 2.5x as many people because the offer is so much more compelling. From there, you are able to charge a 4x higher price up front. The end result is 2.5 x 2.5 x 4 = 22.4x more cash collected up front. 
> [L19] 
> [L20] Yes, you spent $10,000 to make $112,000. You just made money getting new customers.

## INTRO (What we’re doing + how it applies to your credit program)

### How this applies to your program
Your “brick” = your current assets:
- underwriting knowledge + sequencing logic
- application/checklist system
- live guidance
- templates
- approval optimization
- execution support

The goal is **not** to add “more content.”  
It’s to combine your blocks to increase:
- **Dream Outcome**
- **Likelihood**
…and reduce:
- **Time Delay**
- **Effort/Sacrifice** fileciteturn1file3L3-L4

This is the foundation.

---

## PROBLEM 1 (Start here): “Your thing isn’t valuable” / commoditized offers get compared

### What the doc says is happening
Commoditized offers get compared, which forces **price shopping** and margin death. The fix is a Grand Slam Offer that’s so different the offer does the differentiation for you. fileciteturn1file4L1-L9

### What that looks like in *your* market
Right now, buyers compare:
- DFY funders: “$0 upfront + 10% backend”
- DIY memberships: “pay $15–30K and learn/coach over time”
- generic “funding courses”

If you sound like “credit education / coaching,” you are **comparable** → you lose leverage.

---

## STEP-BY-STEP APPLICATION TO YOUR PROGRAM (Problem 1)

### Step 1 — Pick the *top-of-market* avatar
You already did: **720–750+ score operators**.  
That’s your “starving crowd” equivalent (high ability + high desire). fileciteturn1file2L9-L12

### Step 2 — Define the Dream Outcome in one line (no mechanism yet)
For this avatar, the dream isn’t “better credit.” It’s:

> **$100K–$250K+ deployable capital, fast, without paying a funder tax.**

### Step 3 — Make it incomparable by attacking “the bottom” of the value equation
The doc’s key: Pros win by making it **more effortless and immediate** (not just promising more). fileciteturn1file3L3-L4

So your incomparable wedge is:
- **Execution-assisted** (reduces effort)
- **90-day plan** (reduces time delay)
- **Pre-qualification** (increases likelihood)

### Step 4 — Your offer skeleton for Problem 1 (incomparable)
Keep it brutally simple:

**Headline:**  
> **$100K–$250K+ approved credit in 90 days — for 720–750+ operators.**

**Incomparability line:**  
> **We build the first stack with you (execution-assisted), then hand you the system to repeat without funders.**

This is how you stop being “a course / coaching / membership” and become **a completion outcome**.

> Title: HOW TO MAKE A GRAND SLAM OFFER Prob.txt

> Content Snippet:
> [L1] Right.
> [L2] It would work. It would also be a nightmare. So I would probably put contingencies like, "Provided
> [L3] you've already spent $10,000 on your existing offer using our structure, the offer you ran was for lead
> [L4] generation, and it was a free offer. These are things that would make it unlikely they would not succeed.
> [L5] If for some reason they hadn't with those stipulations in place, I could probably fix their problem in a few
> [L6] minutes just looking at it - which lowers my risk.
> [L7] 
> [L8] 2f) Hotel + Airfare Perks Guarantee
> [L9] What the Client Gets: If you don't receive value, we will reimburse your product and your hotel + airfare.
> [L10] 
> [L11] My Take: This is technically a "refund of ancillary costs" from our first example. I just love it a lot for
> [L12] workshops and in-person experiences. Normally the event would cost more than the hotel and airfare, so
> [L13] it's like adding an extra $1000 to a guarantee but way more tangible. It's original enough that people like
> [L14] it.
> [L15] 
> [L16] 2g) Wage-Payment Guarantee
> [L17] 
> [L18] What the Client Gets: You offer to pay their hourly rate, whatever that may be, if they don't find your
> [L19] call/session with them valuable.
> [L20] 
> [L21] My Take: This is also an ancillary cost guarantee. If someone ever asks for the wage payment, just ask
> [L22] them for their tax return and divide it by 1,960 (number of working hours at 40 hrs/wk for a year). But
> [L23] no one asking for a refund will actually do that, so you will never actually have to give one of these out.
> [L24] Like ever.
> [L25] 
> [L26] 2h) Release of Service Guarantee
> [L27] 
> [L28] What the Client Gets: You let them out of their contract free of charge.
> [L29] 
> [L30] My Take: This voids a commitment or cancellation fee. If you have a business that has enforceable
> [L31] commitments, contracts or clauses, this can be a powerful guarantee. Better yet, if you are in a business
> [L32] that does not enforce your contracts, then you have nothing to lose by adding the guarantee.
> [L33] 
> [L34] 2i) Delayed Second Payment Guarantee
> [L35] 
> [L36] What the Client Gets: You won't bill them again until after they make or get their first outcome. Ex: Lose
> [L37] your first five pounds ... make your first sale ... get your website live, etc.
> [L38] 
> [L39] My Take: I like this a lot, especially if you have a very systematized process for getting the first result. It
> [L40] gets the prospect moving. It also focuses your team on activating your client. This is a great one when
> [L41] you know what metric or action drives activation (predicting indicator of long term retention) of a client.
> [L42] I've successfully used this guarantee loads of times.
> [L43] 
> [L44] 2j) First Outcome Guarantee
> [L45] 
> [L46] What the Client Gets: You continue to pay their ancillary costs (ad spend, hotel, etc) until they reach
> [L47] their first outcome. Example: If you don't make your first sale in 14 days, we will pay for your ad spend
> [L48] until you do.
> [L49] 
> [L50] My Take: Just like the delayed second payment, just centered around a different cost. I personally like this
> [L51] setup a lot. It keeps everyone focused on getting that first dollar over the bridge. Once that one comes
> [L52] across, the second comes soon after.
> [L53] 
> [L54] 2k) Prepayment Guarantee
> [L55] 
> [L56] What the Client Gets: You don't need to guarantee everything you sell. Instead, you can choose to
> [L57] guarantee a specific payment plan or option you want someone to take. In this way, a guarantee
> [L58] incentivizes a desired action or commitment.
> [L59] 
> [L60] My Take: This is super effective if you have a product or service many people doubt they'll complete or
> [L61] succeed with. Offering a guarantee as a function of prepayment, gets them even more committed.

> [L1] 2c) Modified Service Guarantee
> [L2] 
> [L3] What the Client Gets: You give them another Y-long period of service or access to your product/services
> [L4] free of charge. Generally, Y should give them at least twice the duration.
> [L5] 
> [L6] My Take: This is like the service guarantee, but it ties a specific duration to your extended work/
> [L7] involvement. So instead of being on the hook "forever," you're only on the hook for an additional Y period
> [L8] of time. I've seen it work magically and keep the business on the hook for a more finite period of time
> [L9] which may be an easier place for you to start before doing the "all out" Service Guarantee above. This
> [L10] works better for businesses with significant costs of ongoing delivery.
> [L11] 
> [L12] 2d) Credit-based Guarantee
> [L13] 
> [L14] What the Client Gets: You give them back what they paid but in a credit toward any service you offer.
> [L15] 
> [L16] My Take: This is best used during an upsell process to seal the deal on a service they are unsure they will
> [L17] like. They already like what they have, you are trying to sell them more of that. Worst case, they can apply
> [L18] it to the thing they already like. So it maintains goodwill with the customer.
> [L19] 
> [L20] 2e) Personal Service Guarantee
> [L21] 
> [L22] What the Client Gets: You work with them one-on-one, free of charge, until they reach X objective or
> [L23] result.
> [L24] 
> [L25] My Take: This is absolutely one of the strongest guarantees. It's like a service guarantee on crack. You will
> [L26] definitely want to add conditions.
> [L27] 
> [L28] Example Conditions: they must respond back in twenty-four hours, they must use the products you
> [L29] tell them to, they must XYZ. Only if they do that, will you keep working with them one-on-one.
> [L30] 
> [L31] This is especially powerful as you scale and become more edified as a business owner. Can you imagine
> [L32] one of my salespeople saying, "Alex will personally work with you until your offer converts"? Right.
> [L33] It would work. It would also be a nightmare. So I would probably put contingencies like, "Provided
> [L34] you've already spent $10,000 on your existing offer using our structure, the offer you ran was for lead
> [L35] generation, and it was a free offer. These are things that would make it unlikely they would not succeed.
> [L36] If for some reason they hadn't with those stipulations in place, I could probably fix their problem in a few
> [L37] minutes just looking at it - which lowers my risk.
> [L38] 
> [L39] 2f) Hotel + Airfare Perks Guarantee
> [L40] What the Client Gets: If you don't receive value, we will reimburse your product and your hotel + airfare.
> [L41] 
> [L42] My Take: This is technically a "refund of ancillary costs" from our first example. I just love it a lot for
> [L43] workshops and in-person experiences. Normally the event would cost more than the hotel and airfare, so
> [L44] it's like adding an extra $1000 to a guarantee but way more tangible. It's original enough that people like
> [L45] it.
> [L46] 
> [L47] 2g) Wage-Payment Guarantee
> [L48] 
> [L49] What the Client Gets: You offer to pay their hourly rate, whatever that may be, if they don't find your
> [L50] call/session with them valuable.
> [L51] 
> [L52] My Take: This is also an ancillary cost guarantee. If someone ever asks for the wage payment, just ask
> [L53] them for their tax return and divide it by 1,960 (number of working hours at 40 hrs/wk for a year). But
> [L54] no one asking for a refund will actually do that, so you will never actually have to give one of these out.
> [L55] Like ever.

> [L58]

> # 【2†HOW TO MAKE A GRAND SLAM OFFER Prob.txt†file_00000000a8e471f5ad43f1268dce94ce】
> Content source: Source.file
> Title: HOW TO MAKE A GRAND SLAM OFFER Prob.txt
> Citation Marker: fileciteturn3file2
> Mclick Target: "3:2"
> Content Snippet:
> [L1] Oftentimes, dream outcomes at their core
> [L2] reflect an increase in social standing (status)
> [L3] within one's group.
> [L4] 
> [L5] Two Offers, Different Dream Outcomes:
> [L6] The dream outcome value driver is most
> [L7] prominently used when comparing the
> [L8] relative value between two different desires being
> [L9] satisfied.
> [L10] 
> [L11] Different Dream Outcome Example:
> [L12] For many men, making money is more
> [L13] important than getting in shape. Main
> [L14] reason: making money increases their
> [L15] status with men and women more than
> [L16] getting in shape will. This would mean, as a
> [L17] category, men who feel this way will likely
> [L18] put a premium on offers that help achieve
> [L19] the "make money" dream outcome more
> [L20] than the "get in shape" dream outcome.
> [L21] 
> [L22] Two Offers, Same Dream Outcome: When
> [L23] comparing two products or services that
> [L24] satisfy the same desire, the value from the
> [L25] dream outcomes will cancel out (since they are
> [L26] the same). It will be the other three variables
> [L27] that drive the difference in perceived value,
> [L28] and ultimately price.
> [L29] Same Dream Outcome Example: if we
> [L30] have two products or services that both
> [L31] help make someone beautiful, it will be the
> [L32] likelihood of achievement, time delay, and
> [L33] effort required that will differentiate the
> [L34] perceived value of each offer.
> [L35] 
> [L36] Simply put: if two things make someone
> [L37] beautiful, what makes one worth $50,000 and
> [L38] another $5? Answer: The extent of the other
> [L39] three value variables.
> [L40] 
> [L41] #2 Perceived Likelihood of
> [L42] 
> [L43] Achievement (Goal = Increase)
> [L44] 
> [L45] People value certainty. They pay a premium
> [L46] for it. I call this "the perceived likelihood of
> [L47] achievement." In other words, "How likely do
> [L48] I believe it is that I will achieve the result I am
> [L49] looking for if I make this purchase?" In other
> [L50] words, the opposite of risk.
> [L51] 
> [L52] Example: How much would you pay to be
> [L53] a plastic surgeon's 10,000th patient versus
> [L54] their first? Answer: A lot more. Why? Because
> [L55] even though they are technically the same
> [L56] procedure, your perceived likelihood of
> [L57] achievement is much higher. And you're
> [L58] willing to pay a premium for it to decrease your
> [L59] risk
> [L60] 
> [L61] Bottom line: Increasing a prospect's
> [L62] conviction that your offer will "actually"
> [L63] work for them, makes your offer more
> [L64] valuable even though you do the same work.
> [L65] You communicate perceived likelihood of
> [L66] achievement through: social proof (think
> [L67] testimonials), guarantees (think if you don't
> [L68] get x, I'll give you y), and third party authority
> [L69] (think degrees).
> [L70] 
> [L71] Exercise #9: Increasing certainty &
> [L72] 
> [L73] lowering risk
> [L74] 
> [L75] If you haven't already, please scan the QR code
> [L76] to download the exercises for this book
> [L77] 
> [L78] SCAN ME
> [L79] 
> [L80] #3 Time Delay (Goal = Decrease)
> [L81] 
> [L82] Time delay is the time between a client buying
> [L83] and receiving the promised benefit. The shorter
> [L84] the distance between when they purchase and
> [L85] when they receive value/the outcome, the
> [L86] more valuable your services or product is. In
> [L87] other words, speed.
> [L88] 
> [L89] Time Delay Example: It's the reason people
> [L90] will pay $25,000 for immediate results with
> [L91] liposuction, while people will barely pay $29/
> [L92] mo for a gym membership to wait 12 to 24
> [L93] months for results. 12 to 24 months to get
> [L94] what you want is a long time when you can do
> [L95] liposuction and be done in an afternoon. Same
> [L96] outcome. Different speeds. Wildly different
> [L97] prices people are willing to pay.
> [L98] 
> [L99] There are two elements to this driver:
> [L100] 
> [L101] 1. Long-term outcome - how long
> [L102] 
> [L103] it takes for them to get to their
> [L104] 
> [L105] destination
> [L106] 
> [L107] 2. Short-term experience - how long it
> [L108] 
> [L109] takes them to experience some benefit
> [L110] 
> [L111] Ideally: create an emotional win as fast as
> [L112] possible.
> # 【3†HOW TO MAKE A GRAND SLAM OFFER Prob.txt†file_00000000a8e471f5ad43f1268dce94ce】
> Content source: Source.file
> Title: HOW TO MAKE A GRAND SLAM OFFER Prob.txt
> Citation Marker: fileciteturn3file3
> Mclick Target: "3:3"
> Content Snippet:
> [L1] So obey their rules.
> [L2] 
> [L3] C- Complete With A Container Word
> [L4] 
> [L5] The container word denotes that this offer is a bundle of lots of things put together. It's a system. It's
> [L6] something that can't be held up to a commoditized alternative.
> [L7] 
> [L8] Examples: Challenge, Blueprint, Bootcamp, Intensive, Incubator, Masterclass, Program, Detox,
> [L9] Experience, Summit, Accelerator, Fast Track, Shortcut, Sprint, Launch, Slingshot, Catapult,
> [L10] Explosion, System, Getaway, Meetup, Transformation, Mastermind, Launch, Game Plan, Deep Dive,
> [L11] Workshop, Comeback, Rebirth, Attack, Assault, Reset, Solution, Hack, Cheatcode, Liftoff, Etc.
> [L12] 
> [L13] Rhymes and Alliteration (Advanced)
> [L14] 
> [L15] Good rhymes stick in people's minds. Rhyme your program name to win the game. Google "rhyming
> [L16] dictionary" for an easy shortcut. Don't try and force it. It's not a requirement, it's just a "nice-to-have."
> [L17] 
> [L18] Rhyming Examples: Six-Pack Fast Track, 5-Day Book Print Sprint, Marriage Thrive Deep Dive, 12-
> [L19] Week 2-Putt Shortcut, 12-Month No-Debt Reset, Celebrity Butt Shortcut, Get Some Ass Masterclass
> [L20] (just thought it was funny), etc. You get the idea.
> [L21] 
> [L22] An alternative approach to rhyming is to use alliteration when naming your program. Alliteration is
> [L23] when you make all (or most) of the words start with the same letter or sound. This is easier for most
> [L24] people than rhyming. Again, you do not need to rhyme or alliterate. Don't force it.
> [L25]

> # 【4†HOW TO MAKE A GRAND SLAM OFFER Prob.txt†file_00000000a8e471f5ad43f1268dce94ce】
> Content source: Source.file
> Title: HOW TO MAKE A GRAND SLAM OFFER Prob.txt
> Citation Marker: fileciteturn3file4
> Mclick Target: "3:4"
> Content Snippet:
> [L1] Offering a guarantee as a function of prepayment, gets them even more committed. This
> [L2] way, you both win.
> [L3] 
> [L4] Scripting: Imagine someone just agreed to sign up. You then say "Would you rather pay less money today
> [L5] or get all your money back?" They ask for clarification. You reply, "It's $4000. You can either do that in
> [L6] four payments of $1000, or you can prepay the $4000 and we'll guarantee XYZ. People who prepare are more
> [L7] committed and follow through so we like to encourage people to do that with this guarantee." So now, people
> [L8] have an even bigger reason to prepay the service.
> [L9] 
> [L10] 3) Anti-Guarantees
> [L11] 
> [L12] Anti-guarantees are when you explicitly state "all sales are final." You want to own this position. Come
> [L13] up with a creative "reason why" the sales are final. Show a massive exposure or vulnerability on your
> [L14] part that a consumer could immediately understand and think "Yes, that makes sense." These types of
> [L15] guarantees are especially important with items that are consumable or diminish in value once given.
> [L16] What the Client Gets: Access to super exclusive very valuable service/product. Likely, this is a very
> [L17] powerful thing that once seen cannot be unseen, or once used cannot be taken away. Example: a line of
> [L18] code to improve your checkout experience on a website. Once someone received this code, they could try
> [L19] and use it without paying you. Or a series of opening messages for picking up girls, or opening sentences
> [L20] for messaging cold prospects. Things that are very valuable but incredibly easy to steal after they've been
> [L21] seen/understood.
> [L22] 
> [L23] My Take: It implies that the client is going to use it and see an immense benefit thereby exposing the
> [L24] business to vulnerability. It acts as a damaging admission. We have an "all sales are final" policy, but that
> [L25] is because our product is so exclusive and so powerful that once used it cannot be unused." Since it is so
> [L26] standard to have some sort of guarantee, not having one is attention-worthy.
> [L27] 
> [L28] So instead of being wishy-washy, lean into the fact this thing works so well, and is so easy to copy, you
> [L29] must make all sales final. They'll believe you even more if you take this position.
> [L30] 
> [L31] Scripting: "We are going to show you our proprietary process that we are using right now to generate leads
> [L32] in our business. Our funnels, ads, and metrics. We're going to be exposing the inner workings of our business,
> [L33] as a result, all sales are final." Note: strong reason why is needed here. Just make one up that sounds
> [L34] compelling. The more you can show real exposure, the more effective this will be.
> [L35] 
> [L36] Anti-guarantees can also work well with high ticket products and services that require a lot of work or
> [L37] customization. "If you're the type of customer who needs a guarantee before taking a jump, then you are not
> [L38] the type of person we want to work with. We want motivated self-starters who can follow instructions and are
> [L39] not looking for a way out before they even begin. If you are not serious, don't buy it. But if you are, boy are you
> [L40] going to make a killing."
> [L41] 
> [L42] 4) Implied Guarantees
> [L43] 
> [L44] Implied guarantees are performance-based offers. Revshare, profitshare, triggers, ratchets, monetary
> [L45] bonuses, etc are all examples. The concept is the same: if I don't perform, I don't get paid. Unique to this
> [L46] particular structure, it also confers the upside of "If I do a great job, I will be well compensated."

> # 【6†The_Lost_Chapter-Your_First_Avatar.pdf†file_00000000bb7c722fba819b4be7c85f72】
> Content source: Source.file
> Title: The_Lost_Chapter-Your_First_Avatar.pdf
> Citation Marker: fileciteturn3file6
> Mclick Target: "3:6"
> Content Snippet:
> [L1] Step 4b: Reverse-engineer buying process. Actions: My team then recreated this “ideal”
> [L2] buying experience. From this point onwards, we injected two long form high value content
> [L3] pieces to each lead as a part of their buyer journey. And, we increased our total output of
> [L4] content. On top of that, we created a list of our “all time greatest hits” of content to arm the
> [L5] sales team. They then hand select two to three pieces they think could help the prospect. By
> [L6] doing that, they forced them to go through the same buying process that caused our best
> [L7] customers to buy. Note: They were not disguised sales pitches, they were genuinely
> [L8] value-in-advance content. (Like this, hopefully).
> [L9] A Comparison
> [L10] A while back, I considered buying an equity stake in a business services company that served
> [L11] fitness business owners. I spent the morning with the business owner learning about his business
> [L12] metrics. From speaking with him, I discovered that despite the owner serving the same vertical as
> [L13] me, and making the same number of total sales, hewas making 70x less profit (yes, seventy times
> [L14] less!) Spoiler: it wasn’t because we’re brilliant. It’s because figuring out the most valuable customers
> [L15] to sell to works.
> [L16] The difference. They accepted anyone with a pulse and a credit card. As a result, they dealt
> [L17] with high customer churn, high costs of acquisition, low retention rates, and lower satisfaction
> [L18] scores. And it had to be that way. Compared to ours, their advice was generic. On the other hand, we
> [L19] selectively pursued and catered to the highest value customers. We ignored all others. This gave us
> [L20] higher retention, higher gross margins, premium pricing, and lots of repeat business. Same market.
> [L21] Different customer segmentation. Monstrously different results. This stuff matters.
> [L22] Quality > Quantity
> [L23] Many competitors try to recreate our buyer journey. However, they don’t fully commit. They
> [L24] panic, then cut out steps to get more volume. This is often a mistake. In my experience, everytime
> [L25] we removed qualification steps, our lead volume increased, but we made less money. Merging
> [L26] marketing and sales into one acquisition department solved this problem for good. Marketing
> [L27] stopped complaining that the sales team wasn't closing. Sales team stopped complaining that they
> [L28] Copyright © 2021 by ACQUISITION.COM LLC NOT FOR DISTRIBUTION 5<PARSED TEXT FOR PAGE: 7 / 8>wanted more leads. Everyone came together to focus on what mattered: closing lots of valuable
> [L29] deals. We now use the optimal amount of steps to generate the highest return on advertising over the
> [L30] long haul.
> [L31] Example: I would rather pay $5000 to acquire $45,000 than pay $1000 to acquire $5000 (even
> [L32] though it costs five times as much).
> [L33] Knowing your ideal buyer journey forces patience. You see the business holistically, rather
> [L34] than as a widget to be sold to as many people as possible. The ladder is how small newbie
> [L35] entrepreneurs think - don't think like that.
> [L36] To give some context, the average Gym Launch competitor has an LTV of around
> [L37] $6000-$8000. I know because I’ve looked at buying their businesses. Our LTV is north of $45,000.
> [L38] Now despite the LTV being *only* 6-8x higher, the subsequent margins that occur as a result are
> [L39] breathtakingly different. For a moment, I want you to imagine 8x your price, and keeping your costs
> [L40] the same. How much more profit would you be making? Probably a lot. That’s the difference.

> [L1] That’s it. More of the high profit customers. Fewer low profit customers. Rinse. Repeat.
> [L2] When he broke down the math, it became even more obvious. It was Pareto's principle
> [L3] (80/20) on steroids. Twenty percent of customers bring in eighty percent of revenue. If you replace
> [L4] the eighty percent with those high spenders, you grow the business 5x. No small feat, especially in
> [L5] billion dollar companies. I wondered how I could apply this method across our portfolio. Since then,
> [L6] it's become a pillar of our value acceleration method (VAM) at acquisition.com
> [L7] Copyright © 2021 by ACQUISITION.COM LLC NOT FOR DISTRIBUTION 1<PARSED TEXT FOR PAGE: 3 / 8>Finding the Right Customers
> [L8] Earlier, I talked about picking the right market. It's an important strategic business decision.
> [L9] Choosing the perfect avatar is a subset of that larger decision. This is where we become more
> [L10] nuanced about exactly who we serve, and more importantly, who we do not.
> [L11] There are four steps to installing this process. I outline them below. Then, I share what we
> [L12] found after implementing this in Gym Launch. Here are the steps.
> [L13] 1) Survey your customers: Set up a form with the questions below and send it out. Or, for
> [L14] higher engagement, go over it with them live at an event or on a call. Make sure they show
> [L15] they completed it to receive some benefit. Ask them every relevant detail you’d want to
> [L16] know. Here’s an example of questions I would ask business services customers:
> [L17] a) Demographics: Who are they? Age? Gender? Political affiliation? Geographic location?
> [L18] Digital Location? Single/Divorced? Partnered in business or solopreneur?
> [L19] b) Business Stats Before & Current: Revenue? Profit,? # of employees? Churn? Pricing?
> [L20] Products? Customer lifetime value? # of customers? Niche? How long in business?
> [L21] c) Aspirations: What was their goal upon purchasing your services/products? What
> [L22] problem were they trying to solve?
> [L23] d) Buying Process: What's the single biggest reason they bought? Was there a trigger event
> [L24] that caused them to buy? Did they consume any specific piece of content? Was there
> [L25] a specific testimonial they consumed? How many pieces did they consume? When
> [L26] did they first hear about you vs when they bought? Where did they first see us? Did
> [L27] someone refer them?
> [L28] 2) Find your biggest spenders: Sort the replies by the customers you like the most, spent the
> [L29] most, and stayed the longest. Focus on the top 20%. Ignore the rest.
> [L30] 3) See what they have in common: This takes reading through all the answers and using your
> [L31] brain. I know. Thinking is hard. The good news is - your competitors won’t do it - easy
> [L32] advantage. Goal: Come up with the fewest qualifiers they all have in common. Now, list
> [L33] them out. Usually there are three to five qualifiers.
> [L34] 4) Execute: Once you have these answers you’re going to do two important things.
> [L35] a) Speak your new avatar. Be upfront about your customer requirements. Get all
> [L36] advertising to speak directly to them. You will repel the bad customers and attract
> [L37] the good ones. Stop selling anyone who does not meet your ideal customer
> [L38] Copyright © 2021 by ACQUISITION.COM LLC NOT FOR DISTRIBUTION 2<PARSED TEXT FOR PAGE: 4 / 8>requirements. Seriously, stop it.

> [L25] How to Use This Chapter to Get More High Quality Leads Immediately
> [L26] Copyright © 2021 by ACQUISITION.COM LLC NOT FOR DISTRIBUTION 6<IMAGE FOR PAGE: 7 / 8><PARSED TEXT FOR PAGE: 8 / 8>You have three actions to do at the end of this chapterto get more leads. First, survey your

> [L28] value customers. Finally, use this information to change your messaging in your advertising, and
> [L29] re-engineer a sales process for them specifically.
> [L30] Over the long haul, this increases the quality and quantity of your lead flow. It also increases
> [L31] your average customer value as you are weeding out all the less-than-qualified potential customers up
> [L32] front.
> [L33] Growing a business comes down to selling more customers or making them worth more.
> [L34] This chapter accomplishes both. You get more clients because your marketing becomes more
> [L35] tailored. And, you make your clients worth more by exclusively selling the highest value people. It
> [L36] feels like cheating because it's so obvious. But here’s the great part - no one does it. Getting this step

## 🤖 ChatGPT

Yes — let’s iron the **offer** out *the way your TXT file teaches it*: by building it from the **Value Equation variables** + **wrapper/“container word”** + **guarantee structure**. fileciteturn3file2L22-L34 fileciteturn3file3L3-L11

We’ll do this **step-by-step**, starting at the beginning:

---

# OFFER BUILD — STEP 1: DREAM OUTCOME

Your TXT says: when two offers have the **same dream outcome**, the difference in value comes from the other 3 variables (likelihood, time delay, effort). fileciteturn3file2L22-L34  
So we still must define Dream Outcome cleanly, but we will win via the other levers.

### Dream Outcome for *your avatar* (720–750+ operator)
> **$100K–$250K+ in approved deployable personal + business credit in 90 days**  
> **without paying a 10% backend “funder tax” and without becoming dependent on a membership.**

This is the “what they want” statement.

**We’ll lock this as the core.**

---

# OFFER BUILD — STEP 2: WRAPPER (CONTAINER WORD)

Your TXT explicitly recommends using a “container word” so it feels like a **system bundle** that can’t be compared to commoditized alternatives. fileciteturn3file3L3-L11

Pick ONE of these containers (my recommendation starred):

- **Capital Stack Sprint** ⭐  
- Funding Fast Track  
- Approval Accelerator  
- Capital Engineering Intensive  

So the offer becomes:

> **The Capital Stack Sprint** — a 90-day execution-assisted system.

This is how we avoid sounding like “course/coaching/membership.”

## 🤖 ChatGPT

Great question — and you’re right to flag it.  
Let’s be **precise, truthful, and regulator-safe**.

Short answer: **“Capital” is not misleading here *if* it’s properly qualified** — but **unqualified use would be sloppy** and you’re right not to accept that.

Let’s walk this **step by step**, starting with first principles.

---

## STEP 1 — IS “CAPITAL” FACTUALLY WRONG?

**No.**  
In finance, *capital* includes:
- Cash
- Credit facilities
- Revolving lines
- Term loans
- Trade credit

**Approved credit = committed capital access**, not hypothetical money.

Banks, PE firms, and CFOs routinely refer to:
- “Available capital”
- “Capital stack”
- “Cost of capital”

…even when funds are not yet drawn.

So **capital ≠ cash**, and that’s standard.

But…

---

## STEP 2 — WHERE IT *BECOMES* MISLEADING

It becomes misleading **only if**:
- You imply immediate cash-in-hand  
- You blur the distinction between *approval* and *deployment*
- You market it like “we wire you money”

And you’re explicitly **not** doing that.

Your avatar already says:
> “a defined use for $100K–$250K+ in deployable capital”

That word — **deployable** — is doing critical ethical work.

---

## STEP 3 — THE REAL ISSUE IS NOT ETHICS, IT’S CLARITY

The real risk is **market confusion**, not deception.

So the solution is **not to abandon “capital”**, but to **anchor it properly**.

You do this by **pairing terms**, not swapping them.

---

## STEP 4 — THE CORRECT, CLEAN LANGUAGE (THIS MATTERS)

Here are **truth-safe, top-of-market phrases** that solve this completely:

### Preferred phrasing (ranked)

1. **“Approved capital access (credit-based)”** ✅ BEST  
2. **“Committed credit capital”**  
3. **“Institutional credit capital”**  
4. **“Approved deployable credit”**

Avoid:
- “Cash”
- “Funds”
- “Money in your account”

You don’t need them.

---

## STEP 5 — REFINED OFFER LANGUAGE (100% CLEAN)

Here is the **corrected, non-misleading version** of the offer core:

> **We engineer $100K–$250K+ in approved, deployable credit capital within 90 days — and transfer the underwriting system so you can repeat it without funders.**

That sentence is:
- Factually accurate
- Legally clean
- Sophisticated
- Impossible to confuse with DFY “cash” offers

---

## STEP 6 — WHY THIS ACTUALLY INCREASES AUTHORITY

Top-of-market operators:
- Understand capital ≠ cash
- Respect precision
- Distrust “money now” language

So this wording **filters UP**, not down.

Low-quality prospects will say:
> “Wait, so it’s credit?”

High-quality prospects will say:
> “Yes — obviously.”

That’s exactly what you want.

---

## VERDICT

- ❌ You were right to reject sloppy usage
- ✅ “Capital” is correct **when qualified**
- ✅ Pairing “capital + credit” is the clean solution
- ✅ This strengthens trust and disqualifies junk leads

> Here’s how i went from $0 to $30k/day (just copy me)

> So, I've gone from zero to about $30,000 a day by the age of 23 without selling any courses. Granted, I did used to sell courses in the past. If you go back on this YouTube channel, you'll see that I used to sell courses. I don't anymore. And the thing that actually makes me $30,000 a day as of right now is not from a course. I don't have any courses to sell. And I've done it without anything to do with AI or building a personal brand or any of the new hypy ways to make money online that you see out there as of right now. Now, by the way, I'm not saying AI is hypy in any way. I think it's here to stay, but I am the least technical person you could ever meet. You can hold me to that. So, quite frankly, I'm very far behind in the AI world because I just don't understand half of it now. And in this video, I'm not going to tell you what you should do. I'm not going to preach in any way. All I'm going to do is just explain how I make my money and how I got from zero to about $30,000 a day without any again of the trendy stuff that you're seeing is being pushed by all of the online gurus as of right now in this space. So, just one other thing before I jump in. I am not going to hype anything up. So if you think this is sounds really boring and this sounds really difficult and it sounds like it's a lot of work, it's because it is. Again, I don't have a course, so I'm not going to try and make this sound sexy. It serves me in no way to make it sound sexier than actually what it is. I'll just tell you exactly my path from 0 to $30,000 a day as of right now. And hopefully this time next year I can say 0 to $60,000 a day and so on and so forth. So to begin with, as I said, you if you go back on this YouTube channel, you will see I used to post a ton. I quite frankly hate watching those old videos, but I made a ton of them. And at the time, I was I had a course teaching people how to create their own ecom agency. And what I did was I had the smart idea. The course business that I had at the time was scalable. And I really wanted to scale, but I also knew at the time YouTube as a vehicle, again, just me being naive and stupid. Quite frankly, I thought YouTube also wasn't scalable. And quite frankly, I wasn't in control of the scale. And that's the thing that I didn't like. I don't think necessarily it was that I didn't think YouTube wouldn't scale. I wasn't in control and you're not. If you're building a personal brand, you have organic of some sort, you're technically not in control over your growth each and every month. In other words, if you do more inputs or, you know, make more videos or do more posts or whatever it is, it doesn't directly correlate to more sales calls. In my case, I have a service- based business, so I sell people over the phone into our service. So, I needed to generate more sales calls to be able to do more business. If I made more YouTube videos, it may increase the volume of bookings that we got. But it would only be number one, incrementally, unless we had a viral video, and you can tell by the channel, not good at making viral videos. And secondly, there was no direct input that I could make where I could tangibly say, if I do X, I'm going to get Y out of the system. And so in my mind, it made it very difficult to scale. And I still do believe that to this day. So I then transitioned to paid ads. I was like, okay, I need to run paid ads for this offer because obviously with paid ads, you are completely in control over your scale. If you spend more on ads, you make more money if the ads don't break at scale. And if they do, it's because your ads aren't good enough. So, you're in control cuz you can just make the ads better, which allows you to make more money. Right? Now, the problem was that I had a coaching offer, as I said. And so, when I took that offer to the marketplace on ads, which bear in mind, people with through the ad platform have no idea who you are. People who follow you know who you are. They follow you. That's the whole part of personal brand. And so the offer that I had at the time that I was selling to my personal brand, to my organic audience, it was a terrible offer, but the reason why people bought it was because they liked Kai. They liked me. And so I again naively thought, "Oh my gosh, I have a great offer. It works so well, etc. So I'm going to take this to paid ads and I'm going to scale it up and I'm going to make a ton of money." Lo and behold, you probably guessed it, I made nothing. I lost a ton of money. In fact, my close rate on cold traffic was 5%. It was abysmal. And what I realized was two things. Number one, my offer was just terrible. But more importantly, number two, nobody actually wanted what it is that I was selling. So in my front-end ads, I was being quite ambiguous at the time. It was a done with you coaching offer, but I didn't imply it was done for you, but let's say I didn't say it was done with you. So everybody booked in a sales call. So my front-end ad metrics actually looked really good. My cost per call was I can't remember. It was close to around $100 maybe. It was very, very cheap. And so I was like, this is great. However, when I started taking the sales calls, which by the way, in the early days, this is a big misconception most people have. They try and hire a closer before they validated their sales process. And there's a multitude of reasons as to why I personally believe that's a big no no. But going back to the story, I was taking all the sales calls, and I still would do that today if I was to go back. And on the sales calls, I very quickly realized, as I said, nobody wanted the actual product that it is that I was selling. Everybody wanted a done for you service which at the time basically just so for some context I was helping agency owners and coaches generate more sales calls by getting them or by helping them run paid ads. But again the way I was helping them run paid ads was through the coaching offer. And what I found as I said with people who didn't know who we were, they didn't care for us. They had no affiliation to us. Those guys were just not interested in a coaching offer. They just wanted somebody to come in and do it for them. And so this was my first ever like really big and painful learning lesson which was most people from what I see and this is what I did. They try and build a product that they want to deliver. They try and build a offer that they feel comfortable delivering. The problem with that is if you feel comfortable delivering it that's great. But just because you feel comfortable doing it doesn't mean to say somebody else wants it. And so what I see so much is people try and take this offer or this fulfillment, this business that they want to run, and then they try and sell other people on either becoming a client of that business or wanting to buy that product, whatever it is, whatever industry you're in. And that's what I did. And I lost a ton of money. And as I said before, going back to the story, everybody wanted done for you. And I personally at the time really didn't want to build a done for you business. I wanted to be coaching. I thought it was more scalable. I thought it'd be easier for me to deliver, etc., etc. I really didn't want to be done for you. But it was so obvious to me that everybody wanted it that the lesson that I learned was instead of me trying to force onto the market the thing that I wanted to sell, instead it was so much more profitable for me to just see what the market wanted and then figure out how to deliver that. So in either instance, you've got a problem. There's two problems. In the first instance, you got to try and get people to want to buy your thing. In the second instance, you you've got to try and figure out how to deliver the thing the market actually wants. And again, from my experience with my skills, and this is why I'm saying I'm not saying you should do this in any way, just what I experienced, it was much easier for me at least to just figure out a way to deliver the thing the market already wanted and then sell that. Because once I was able to say, okay, we can do done for you, you know, to to the prospect who booked in the sales call, everyone closed. And for context, when I had the original product that nobody wanted, I was closing at a 5% close rate. Horrific. And again, we lost a ton of money during that period. Now looking back I realize I just paid at the time it was probably 30 grand, 40 grand, 50 grand, I can't remember exactly, but it was a lot of money at the time. But now I just think I just paid a 30 to 50 grand tuition fee to figure out the offer that was actually going to work. So it's not a loss or at least I don't see it as a loss. Maybe that's just me trying to rationalize to

> # 【1†HOW TO MAKE A GRAND SLAM OFFER Prob.txt†file_00000000a8e471f5ad43f1268dce94ce】
> Content source: Source.file
> Title: HOW TO MAKE A GRAND SLAM OFFER Prob.txt
> Citation Marker: fileciteturn5file1
> Mclick Target: "5:1"
> Content Snippet:
> [L1] INTRO
> [L2] 
> [L3] "Outsized returns often come from betting against conventional wisdom, and conventional wisdom is
> [L4] usually right. Given a 10 percent chance of a 100 times payoff, you should take that bet every time.
> [L5] But you're still going to be wrong nine times out of ten ... We all know that if you swing for the fences,
> [L6] you're going to strike out a lot, but you're also going to hit some home runs. The difference between
> [L7] 
> [L8] baseball and business, however, is that baseball has a truncated outcome distribution. When you swing,
> [L9] 
> [L10] no matter how well you connect with the ball, the most runs you can get is four. In business, every once in
> [L11] 
> [L12] a while, when you step up to the plate, you can score 1,000 runs. This long-tailed distribution of returns
> [L13] is why it's important to be bold. Big winners pay for so many experiments."
> [L14] 
> [L15] -Jeff Bezos
> [L16] 
> [L17] As entrepreneurs, we make bets everyday. We are gamblers - gambling our hard-earned money on
> [L18] labor, inventory, rent, marketing, etc., all with the hopes of a higher payout. Oftentimes, we lose. But,
> [L19] sometimes, we win and win BIG. However, there is a difference between gambling in business and
> [L20] gambling in a casino. In a casino, the odds are stacked against you. With skill, you can improve them,
> [L21] but never beat them. In contrast, in business, you can improve your skills to shift the odds in your favor.
> [L22] Simply stated, with enough skill, you can become the house.
> [L23] 
> [L24] After beginning a series of books on acquisition, it became apparent that I could not talk about
> [L25] any other topic without first addressing the offer: the starting point of any conversation to initiate a
> [L26] transaction with a customer. What you are literally providing them in exchange for their money. That's
> [L27] where it all begins.
> [L28] 
> [L29] This book is about how to make profitable offers. Specifically, how to reliably turn advertising dollars
> [L30] into (enormous) profits using a combination of pricing, value, guarantees, and naming strategies. I call
> [L31] the proper combination of these components: a Grand Slam Offer.
> [L32] 
> [L33] I chose this term partially in homage to the above quote from Amazon founder Jeff Bezos and because,
> [L34] like a grand slam in baseball, a Grand Slam Offer is both very good and very rare. Additionally, to
> [L35] extend the baseball metaphor, it takes no more effort to make a Grand Slam Offer than to strike out. The
> [L36] difference is dictated by the skill of the marketer and how well he connects his offer with his audience's
> [L37] desires. In business you can have so-so offers: the "singles" and "doubles' that keep the game going, pay
> [L38] 
> [L39] 
> [L40] the bills, and keep the lights on. But, unlike baseball, where a grand slam scores a maximum of four runs,
> [L41] a Grand Slam Offer in the business world, can score you a thousand-fold payoff and result in a world
> [L42] where you never need to work again. It would be like connecting with the ball so well during one single
> [L43] at bat that you automatically win every World Series for the next hundred years.
> [L44] 
> [L45] It takes years of practice to make something as complicated as hitting a major league fastball into the
> [L46] bleachers look effortless. Your stance, vision, prediction, ball speed, bat speed, and hip placement all
> [L47] must be perfect. In marketing and customer acquisition (the process of getting new clients), there are
> [L48] just as many variables that must all align to truly "knock it out of the park." But with enough practice and
> [L49] enough skill, you can turn the wild world of acquisition, which will throw curveballs at you everyday,
> [L50] into a home run derby, knocking offer after offer out of the stadium.

> # 【3†HOW TO MAKE A GRAND SLAM OFFER Prob.txt†file_00000000a8e471f5ad43f1268dce94ce】
> Content source: Source.file
> Title: HOW TO MAKE A GRAND SLAM OFFER Prob.txt
> Citation Marker: fileciteturn5file3
> Mclick Target: "5:3"
> Content Snippet:
> [L1] In short, if everyone wants your super valuable thing, then you will get lots of people to
> [L2] shell over their cash to get it. This beats haggling over pennies with very few people who don't want what
> [L3] you have. Better, right? Right.
> [L4] 
> [L5] This concludes the intro. Now we go into the problems and solutions most businesses have with their
> [L6] offers.
> [L7] 
> [L8] 
> [L9] 
> [L10] 
> [L11] HOW TO MAKE A GRAND SLAM OFFER 
> [L12] 
> [L13] Problem #1: You Sell a Commodity —> Solution #1: Differentiate “Think different.” - Steve Jobs   
> [L14] 
> [L15] Value-Driven vs. Price-Driven Purchases 
> [L16] 
> [L17] Having a Grand Slam Offer helps with all three of the requirements for growth: getting more customers, getting them to pay more, and getting them to do so more times. 
> [L18] 
> [L19] How? It allows you to differentiate yourself from the marketplace. In other words, it allows you to sell your product based on VALUE not on PRICE. 
> [L20] 
> [L21] Commoditized = Price Driven Purchases (race to the bottom) 
> [L22] Differentiated = Value Driven Purchases (sell in a category of one with no comparison. Yes, market matters, which I’ll explain in the next chapter) 
> [L23] 
> [L24] A commodity, as I define it, is a product available from many places. For that reason, it’s prone to purchases based on “price” instead of “value.” If all products are “equal,” then the cheapest one is the most valuable by default. In other words, if a prospect compares your product to another and thinks “these are pretty much the same, I’ll buy the cheaper one,” then they commoditized you. How embarrassing! But really . . . it’s one of the worst experiences a value-driven entrepreneur can have. This is a massive problem for the entrepreneur because commodities are valued at the point of market efficiency. 
> [L25] 
> [L26] This means that the marketplace drives the price down through competition until the margins are just enough to keep the lights on: “just enough” to become a slave to their business. The business makes “just enough” to justify the owner waiting anxiously for things to “turn around,” and by the time that lie is realized . . . they are in too deep to pivot (at least, until now). 
> [L27] 
> [L28] A Grand Slam Offer solves this problem.  
> [L29] 
> [L30] Exercise #1: Price vs Value Driven Purchases   
> [L31] 
> [L32] What Does A Grand Slam Offer Change? 
> [L33] Let’s start by defining a Grand Slam Offer. 
> [L34] 
> [L35] It’s an offer you present to the marketplace that cannot be compared to any other product or service available. This forces a value rather than a price based buying decision. 
> [L36] It combines an attractive promotion, an unmatchable value proposition, a premium price, and an unbeatable guarantee with a money model (payment terms) that allows you to get paid to get new customers . . . forever removing the cash constraint on business growth. 
> [L37] 
> [L38] In other words, it allows you to sell in a “category of one,” or, to apply another great phrase, to “sell in a vacuum.” The resulting purchasing decision for the prospect is now between your product and nothing. So you can sell at whatever price you get the prospect to perceive, not in comparison to anything else. As a result, it gets you more customers, at higher ticket prices, for less money. 
> [L39] 
> [L40] If you like fancy marketing terms, it breaks down like this: 
> [L41] 1)Increased Response Rates (think clicks) 
> [L42] 2)Increased Conversion (think sales) 
> [L43] 3)Premium Prices (think charging a lot of money). 
> [L44] Having a Grand Slam Offer increases your response rates to advertisements (aka more people will click or take an action on an advertisement they see containing a Grand Slam Offer).

> # 【5†HOW TO MAKE A GRAND SLAM OFFER Prob.txt†file_00000000a8e471f5ad43f1268dce94ce】
> Content source: Source.file
> Title: HOW TO MAKE A GRAND SLAM OFFER Prob.txt
> Citation Marker: fileciteturn5file5
> Mclick Target: "5:5"
> Content Snippet:
> [L1] New Grand Slam Offer Way (Differentiated, Incomparable) (Value-Driven) Grand Slam Offer: Pay one time. (No recurring fee. No retainer.) Just cover ad spend. I’ll generate leads and work your leads for you. And only pay me if people show up. And I’ll guarantee you get 20 people in your first month, or you get your next month free. I’ll also provide all the best practices from other businesses like yours. 
> [L2] 
> [L3] ●Daily sales coaching for your staff 
> [L4] ●Tested scripts 
> [L5] ●Tested price points and offers to swipe and deploy 
> [L6] ●Sales recordings . . . and everything else you need to sell and fulfill your customers. 
> [L7] 
> [L8] I’ll give you the entire play book for (insert industry), absolutely free just for becoming a client. In a nutshell, I'm feeding people into your business, showing you, exactly, how to sell them so that you can get the highest prices, which means that you make the most money possible . . . sound fair enough? 
> [L9] 
> [L10] It’s clear these are drastically different offers . . . but so what? Where’s the money!? Let’s compare both in the below chart.       Breakdown: You spend the same amount of money for the same eyeballs. Then, you get 2.5x more people to respond to your advertisement because it’s a more compelling offer. From there, you close 2.5x as many people because the offer is so much more compelling. From there, you are able to charge a 4x higher price up front. The end result is 2.5 x 2.5 x 4 = 22.4x more cash collected up front. 
> [L11] 
> [L12] Yes, you spent $10,000 to make $112,000. You just made money getting new customers. Comparison: Remember the old way, the way you lost half the ad spend up front? With the new way, you are making more money and getting more customers. This means that your cost to acquire a customer is so cheap (relative to how much you make) that your limiting factor becomes your ability to do the work you already love doing. Cash flow and acquiring customers is no longer your bottleneck because it’s 22.4x more profitable than the old model. Yup. You read that right. This is the part in the action movie where you walk away from an explosion in slow motion. This is the exact Grand Slam Offer we used with our software business that serves agencies. The numbers can become wild . . . fast. 
> [L13] 
> [L14] I know 22.4x better sounds unreasonable, but that's the point. If you play the same game everyone else does, you’ll get the same results everyone else does (mediocre). When you get this right, the results are, well . . . unbelievable. Summary Points 
> [L15] ●The problem with commoditization and being the same as others 
> [L16] ●Price-based buying decisions vs Value-based buying decisions 
> [L17] ●The value of a grand slam offer is that you aren’t compared to anything else available in the marketplace 
> [L18] ●Real world impact of making a grand slam offer 
> [L19] 
> [L20] Problem #2: Bad Customers —> Solution #2: A Starving Crowd The seed that fell on good soil represents those who truly hear and understand God’s word and produce a harvest of thirty, sixty, or even a hundred times as much as had been planted!” - Matthew 13:23 (NLT)     
> [L21] 
> [L22] A grand slam offer presented to the wrong audience will fall on deaf ears. On the other hand, if you get the market right, you can get everything else wrong and still make money. This chapter focuses on picking the right market.
> # 【6†HOW TO MAKE A GRAND SLAM OFFER Prob.txt†file_00000000a8e471f5ad43f1268dce94ce】
> Content source: Source.file
> Title: HOW TO MAKE A GRAND SLAM OFFER Prob.txt
> Citation Marker: fileciteturn5file6
> Mclick Target: "5:6"
> Content Snippet:
> [L1] This means that the marketplace drives the price down through competition until the margins are just enough to keep the lights on: “just enough” to become a slave to their business. The business makes “just enough” to justify the owner waiting anxiously for things to “turn around,” and by the time that lie is realized . . . they are in too deep to pivot (at least, until now). 
> [L2] 
> [L3] A Grand Slam Offer solves this problem.  
> [L4] 
> [L5] Exercise #1: Price vs Value Driven Purchases   
> [L6] 
> [L7] What Does A Grand Slam Offer Change? 
> [L8] Let’s start by defining a Grand Slam Offer. 
> [L9] 
> [L10] It’s an offer you present to the marketplace that cannot be compared to any other product or service available. This forces a value rather than a price based buying decision. 
> [L11] It combines an attractive promotion, an unmatchable value proposition, a premium price, and an unbeatable guarantee with a money model (payment terms) that allows you to get paid to get new customers . . . forever removing the cash constraint on business growth. 
> [L12] 
> [L13] In other words, it allows you to sell in a “category of one,” or, to apply another great phrase, to “sell in a vacuum.” The resulting purchasing decision for the prospect is now between your product and nothing. So you can sell at whatever price you get the prospect to perceive, not in comparison to anything else. As a result, it gets you more customers, at higher ticket prices, for less money. 
> [L14] 
> [L15] If you like fancy marketing terms, it breaks down like this: 
> [L16] 1)Increased Response Rates (think clicks) 
> [L17] 2)Increased Conversion (think sales) 
> [L18] 3)Premium Prices (think charging a lot of money). 
> [L19] Having a Grand Slam Offer increases your response rates to advertisements (aka more people will click or take an action on an advertisement they see containing a Grand Slam Offer). 
> [L20] 
> [L21] If you pay the same amount for eyeballs but 1) more people respond, 2) more of those responses buy, and 3) they buy for higher prices, your business grows. 
> [L22] 
> [L23] Here’s the key takeaway from all this: a business does the same work in both cases (with a commoditized or a Grand Slam Offer). The fulfillment is the same. But if one business uses a Grand Slam Offer and another uses a “commodity” offer, the Grand Slam Offer makes that business appear as if it has a totally different product — and that means a value-driven, versus price-driven, purchase. 
> [L24] 
> [L25] If you have a “commodity” offer, you will compete on price (having a price-driven purchase versus a value-driven purchase). 
> [L26] 
> [L27] Your Grand Slam Offer, however, forces a prospect to stop and think differently to assess the value of your differentiated product. Doing this establishes you as your own category, which means it’s too difficult to compare prices, which means you re-calibrate the prospect’s value-meter.   
> [L28] 
> [L29] Exercise #2: What does a new offer affect? 
> [L30] 
> [L31] Real Life Case Study: Grand Slam Offer Before & After (Skip if you’re pressed for time) Three sentence backstory . . . we own a software company that advertising agencies use to work leads for their customers. Using our software, agencies transform their offer from a commoditized offer of lead generation services to a Grand Slam Offer of “pay for performance.” Let me show you the multiplicative effect it has on the revenue of the business. **While rounded for illustration’s sake, these values are based on the real numbers a lead generation agency selling services to brick and mortar businesses experience** Old Commoditized Way (Price-Driven) — Race to the bottom Commoditized Offer: $1,000 down, then $1,000/mo retainer for agency services       
> [L32] 
> [L33] Breakdown: At .5 to 1 return on advertising spend, you lose money getting customers.

> [L3] business. 32 turnarounds in two years. Things looked good until I lost all my money a second time due to
> [L4] a processing mishap (story in $100M Offers). Yes, I've lost all my money twice. So I know what it's like to
> [L5] have nothing with your back against the wall. I failed/closed nine businesses in my career before making
> [L6] my first really big one.
> [L7] 
> [L8] Credibility
> [L9] My first really big one - Gym Launch - was a gym licensing company. We grew it to over 5000+
> [L10] locations. I sold it (and its sister company Prestige Labs) for $46.2M at age 31. I had taken out
> [L11] ~$40,000,000 in owner earnings before selling it. I crossed $100M in net worth by age 32. I'm now an
> [L12] investor with a portfolio of companies that do $200,000,000 per year.
> [L13] What kept me alive in the early days - and has kept my companies growing since then - has been my
> [L14] 
> [L15] ability to make grand slam offers (the subject of this workbook).
> [L16] 
> [L17] Intention
> [L18] 
> [L19] My investment portfolio is my day job. Making content for entrepreneurs is my side hustle. I make
> [L20] the content to attract business owners so that I can invest in their companies and help them grow. I
> [L21] usually only do deals with companies over ~$3,000,000 in annual profit. For everyone else, I just hope
> [L22] your life gets better from these lessons that took me far too much pain to learn.
> [L23] 
> [L24] 
> [L25] 
> [L26] Problem B: Why Business Owners are Broke - Terrible
> [L27] 
> [L28] Offers -> Solution B: Grand Slam Offers
> [L29] 
> [L30] How I learned about making grand slam offers
> [L31] 
> [L32] When I was 23 years old, I paid for a weekend workshop from a business guru. I had never run a
> [L33] business before, nor had I marketed. I didn't know how to make money. The guru, seeing how lost I
> [L34] felt, called me over during the break. He said "do you want to know the secret to sales ...? " I nodded and
> [L35] leaned in " ... make people an offer so good they'd feel stupid saying no."
> [L36] This is where I learned that if I just made people incredible offers, I could win. I didn't have to be an
> [L37] amazing marketer. I didn't have to be an amazing salesperson. I could just make incredible offers - which
> [L38] I have coined "grand slam offers" - and make money.
> [L39] 
> [L40] MAKE PEOPLE AN OFFER SO GOOD THEy
> [L41] WOuLD FEEL STURID SAYINE NG.
> [L42] 
> [L43] What's An Offer Anyways?
> [L44] 
> [L45] The only way to conduct business is through a value exchange, a trade of dollars for value. The offer is
> [L46] what initiates this trade. In a nutshell, the offer is the goods and services you agree to give or provide,
> [L47] how you accept payment, and the terms of the agreement. It is what begins the process of getting
> [L48] customers and making money. It is the first thing any new customer will interact with in your business.
> [L49] Since the offer is what attracts new customers, it is the lifeblood of your business.
> [L50] 
> [L51] The Two Main Problems Most Entrepreneurs Face and How This Book Solves Them
> [L52] 
> [L53] Although you can make the list of problems you face a mile long, which is a great way to stress yourself
> [L54] out, all these problems typically stem from two big kahunas:
> [L55] 
> [L56] 1) Not enough clients
> [L57] 
> [L58] 2) Not enough cash (excess profit at the end of the month)
> [L59] 
> [L60] Making a Grand Slam Offer solves both.

# VIDEO 1 — CORE BREAKDOWN (WHAT ACTUALLY MATTERS)

## The Video’s Real Purpose
Not the offer itself.  
The video teaches **how to THINK about offers** so they explode.

Kai explicitly says:

So we start with the **principles**, not tactics.

---

## PRINCIPLE 1 — PRE-EXISTING DESIRE (THE FOUNDATION)

### What Kai did
He asked 10 ideal clients:
> “If I could snap my fingers and give you anything, what would you want?”

9/10 said the **same thing**.

He didn’t invent desire.  
He **harvested** it.

### The mistake he calls out
Most people:
- Start with what their product can do
- Then try to convince the market to want it

That always caps growth.

### Why this matters for you
You already did this *correctly* by defining:
- 720–750+
- Clean file
- Defined use for $100K–$250K+

That avatar already **contains demand**.

📌 **Key insight:**  
If desire is not pre-existing, no framing will save the offer.

---

## PRINCIPLE 2 — ONE-OF-ONE (NOT “DIFFERENT”, BUT UNCOMPARABLE)

This is where most people misunderstand Kai.

### What he does NOT mean
- Fancy branding
- New buzzwords
- Clever naming

### What he DOES mean
An offer that **cannot be mentally compared side-by-side**.

Kai gives two levers:

#### Lever A — Unique Outcome  
Same category, but a **step beyond** what others promise.

Example:
- Others: “more patients”
- Him: “scale from 1 → 3 locations in 12 months”

#### Lever B — Unique Volume  
Same outcome, but a **bigger, specific number**.

Example:
- Others: “get clients”
- Him: “15 new qualified clients per month”

### Critical nuance
The outcome itself may NOT be unique.  
The **packaging + scope** is what breaks comparison.

📌 **Key insight:**  
One-of-one means *“I can’t evaluate this using the same ruler.”*

---

## PRINCIPLE 3 — PROOF MUST EXCEED THE PROMISE

This is the **non-negotiable**.

Kai says (paraphrased):
> “If your proof isn’t bigger than the offer, the offer collapses.”

Why?
- Big promises trigger skepticism
- Bigger proof neutralizes it

This is why:
- Weak operators can’t run bold offers
- Strong operators *should*

📌 **Key insight:**  
Bold offers are earned, not invented.

---

## BONUS PRINCIPLE — NEW WAY TO WIN (THE STEROID)

This is the part almost everyone skips.

Two companies can promise the **same result**.

The one that wins is the one that:
- Explains **why past attempts failed**
- Introduces a **new mechanism** for success

Kai’s exact logic:
> New method → higher certainty → higher conversion

Not because it’s magical —  
but because it **reframes failure**.

📌 **Key insight:**  
Your method must explain the past AND justify the future.

---

# VIDEO 1 — FINAL DEBRIEF (IN ONE FORMULA)

Here is the **implicit formula** Kai is teaching:

> **Pre-Existing Desire**  
> → framed as a **non-comparable outcome**  
> → supported by **proof that exceeds the claim**  
> → delivered through a **new, superior mechanism**

That’s it.  
Everything else is noise.

# ❓ **1) Do gamers *as a group* have bad credit?**

There’s **no direct publicly available research** showing the average credit scores of gamers vs non-gamers.  
So claiming “gamers have bad credit” is **an assumption, not data-backed**.

Here’s what *is* supported:

📍 **Gaming is massive:**  
The global online gaming market was valued at **$225B+ in 2025** and is growing rapidly, with hundreds of millions of players worldwide (e.g., ~668M gamers in China alone). citeturn0search2

BUT — that says nothing about:
- players’ income levels
- credit profiles
- financial stability

📍 There is **no reliable public statistic** on average credit scores of gamers vs other adults.

👉 So if you want to use credit quality assumptions in your offer, they must come from **your own data** (your lead pool, past clients, etc.), not industry data.

**Conclusion:**  
> The idea that gamers have bad credit is *assumption*, not evidence.

---

# 🎯 **2) Where does the *credit profile risk assumption* usually come from?**

It’s derived from *market realism*, not official data:

- Younger adults historically have lower credit scores on average than older adults — younger people (18–29) tend to have thinner files, shorter histories, and more volatility. (General consumer credit data, not gaming specific.)  
- Many individuals who say “I want capital to escape 9–5” aren’t yet financially disciplined — this is observed behavior among beginner entrepreneurs.

**IMPORTANT:**  
This is **market truth based on real credit ecosystems** (FICO score distributions), but only if you check your own funnel’s performance data.

So if your own lead pool confirms low credit quality, that *is valid real data* for you — just not general population data.

---

✔ Low refund risk

- High-income professionals
- Repeat founders

These markets:
- Value capital outcomes
- *Have better credit foundations*
- Are less skeptical
- Lower CPA + churn

### RISKY: Pure psychographic segments without economic anchor
- “Gamers” without business context
- “Spiritual / starseed” identities
- Hobbyist entrepreneurs

These groups:
- Do not have *pre-existing desire for business credit*
- Might opt out due to skepticism
- Increase educational burden

Hormozi formula:
**Dream outcome × Perceived likelihood / (Time × Effort)** — these risky segments tank *likelihood* and inflate *educational burden*.

You’ll know what the niche wants when you stop guessing and start listening to them directly. The simplest way is to ask them. Have real conversations with ideal prospects or run targeted surveys with open-ended questions. What keeps them up at night? What outcome would they pay for yesterday if you could deliver it? Also, study their behavior: What are they already buying, searching for, or engaged in? Patterns will emerge, and when you hear the same pain or desire echoed across multiple people, that’s your signal. Let them show you the demand before you craft the supply.
